16 - 4 - 1979. Being aggrieved by this refusal the work 
my liad immediately addressed a letter to the dis 
ciplinary authority pointing out the approach of the 
cnquiry officer and informing the said authority that 
he was being denied the opportunity to examine the 
documents which were relevant. The Organisation 
further contended that during the enquiry proceedings

the cnquiry officer scmetimes introduced his own

of the said application . According to the Organisa 
tion , on 13 - 3 - 1981, the workman made an applica 
tion for deleting questions in respect of nisconduct 
which was not included in ihe charge -sheet but the 
enquiry officer over ruled the objection . The Orga 
nisation also made grievance that the workman want 
ed to examine Shri Vatkar and Shri B . R , Şanade as 
defence witnesses but due to the pressure frought an 
them by the officers of the Bank the said witnesses 
refused to attend the enquiry and hence the only 
alternative left to the workman was to prove his case 
by way of documentary evidence but the enquiry 
officer refused the request of the workman to direct 
the management to produce over time sheets called 
for hy him . The Organisation maintained that prin 
cinles of natural justice were violated in as much as 
tle workman was refused legal assistancc . his request 
for production of documents was rejected without 
considering the question of relevancy of those docu 
ments pod without hearing the workinan and that the 
defence witnesses were pressurised to stay away from 
the enquiry , The Organisation further contended that 
in the phience of any direct evidence or evidence of 
handwriting expert it was wrong to conclude that the

to be represented by a lawyer. Both the enquiry offi 
cers did nothing wrong and did what they were expec 
ted to do especially in view of the specific provision 
quoted above contained in sub - pasa ( a ) of para 
graph 19 . 12 of the Binartite Settlement , As per the 
said provision , permission to be defended by a law 
yer could only be given by the management of the 
Bank and the enquiry officer had no discretion in 
the matter. 

15 . It will also be seen from the records of both 
the enquiries that no prejudice was caused and the 
dcfence of the workman was not handicanped on 
account of refusal of permission to be defended by 
a lawyer . In the first enquiry . the Union represen 
tative Shri Pathan , who represented the work 
man and in the second enquiry the work 
man himself carried out the cross -examina 
tion of the management witnesses thoroughly . As a 
matter of fact, the cross examination of Shri Jachak , 
who was one of the management witnesses in the 
second enquiry ran into 117 typed pages. There is 
therefore no substance in the contention that he 
cause the workman was not permitted to he assisted 
by a lawyer the cnruries stood vitiated for want of 
adequate opportunity to the workman to defend 
himself .

It is however pertinent to note that as mentioned

follows : 
" As I have already instructed on 19 -4 - 1982

in cross examination in such a way that

wards. " 
The workman gave a long reply giving reasons for 
his long cross- examination and told the enquiry ofli 
cer that he would continue the cross-examination of 
the witnesses till his satisfaction . The enquiry officer 
however directed the workman to concludo his cross

the cross- examination at that liour , stating that the 
workman was given more and ample time to cross 
examine the witness and that workman was cau

trict his questions to the most relevant facts. He also 
observed that the we ;kman was making abuse if 
the domestic enquiry , Looking to the nature of the 
charge and cross - examination of Shri Jachak it is 
clear that the enquliy officer had already given a 
long rope to the workman in cross examining the 
witness . The workman also jufficiently cross - exa 
mincd Shri Sankpal and wanted to prolong the cross 
examination to the rext date on the ground that he 
wanted to visit a doctor as he was not kecping well 
and also for bringing some documents for further 
cross - examination of Shri Sankpal. He never men 
tioned the documents on the basis of which he 
wanted to further cross oxamine Shri Sankpal, who 
was examined to show that the workman did not 
visit his village for obtaining his signature on 22 - 5 -81, 
and that he had signed the relevant documents long 
beforc the alleged visit of the workman to his vil 
lage . There is thereforc no substance in the con 
tention that the enquiry officer Shri Mithari put a 
abrupt stop to the cross -cxamination of the two wit 
nesses of the management and that the workman 
was prejudiced as a result of abrupt end of the cross 
examination . It will be seen from the record of 
the enquiry proceedings that Shri Mithari was more 
than fair to the workman and allowed him more 
than adequate opportunity to establish his defence . 

22 . The only ground on which bias the attributed 
to the first enquiry officer Shri A . D . Barve was that 
he was a friend of Shri N . D . Phadke, who was 
working as Manager of the Kolhapur Branch from 
Deceinber 1976 to March 1979 and under whom 
the workman was working during the said period . 
There is however nothing on record to show that 
Phadke was enimical towards the workman and was 
interested in falsely implicating the workman in a 


serious charge of forgery . There is not even a 
whisper about it in the entirc cross cxamination of 
Shri Phadke in the onquiry . It is true that the work 
man had objected to Shri Barves appointment as 
an cnquiry officer on the ground that he was a 
close friend of Shri Phadke . But there is nothing 
on rocord to show that Shri A , D . Barve was su 
close to Shri Phadke as to oblige him in establishing 
a false charge against the workman . Shri Barve 
who was examined , has admitted in his cross -exami 
nation before this Tribunal that he knew Shri Phadke 
since 1955 when they were working together in the 
Bombay Main Office . He also admitted that both 
of them had worked in the pune region but be denied 
that Shri Phadke was his personal friend since before 
the enquiry . The workman has also not stcpped in the 
witness box to state on oath that Shri Phadke , Shri 
A , D . Barbe and Shri Mithuri were biased against 
him and were intercsted in falsely implicating him 
in onc misconduct or the other. Nothing was sugges 
ted in the cross -examination of Shri Mithaci from 
which it can be even be vaguely infcrred that Shri 
Mithari was actualed by any malice or bias against 
the workman . It is therefore difficult to accept the 
contention of the workman that the enquiries held 
against him were vitiated on account of the bias of 
the enquiry officers against him . 

23. The contention that the findings of both the 
enquiry otficers are perverse cannot be accepted . 
The Jundings are based on evidence placed before 
them by tne management. The conclusions drawn 
from thc cvidence are reasonably possible . As a 
matter of fact , it was not shown that any finding of 
the enquiry officers is not backed up by evidence 
and does not arise out of it . It is true that there is 
no direct evidence to prove the forgery which is 
basis of the first charge in the first enquiry . But 
there cannot be direct evidence of forgery and it is 
always a matter of inference drawn from other cvi 
dence and circumstances. Shri Ghanekar , the con 
cerned officer disowned the two initials. There was 
no plansible reason why he should do so . It wųs 
ulso established to the satisfaction of the enquiry 
officer that the two initials were different from the 
admitted initials of Shri Ghanckar and the work 
man alone was benefited by the transaction . It was 
ne who got the overtime allowance . Thç inference 
that two initials were forged was reasonably possible . 
All the charges in the cnquiry were duly established 
ufter proper departmental enquiries in which the 
workman was given ample opportunity to defend 
himself .
